# Fresh anonymous dispute adjudication

Read only your execution plan and its allowed reader, including every shared evidence block, completely in untruncated chunks. Confirm the sole absolute output. Tell the controller when reading is complete, then wait for authenticated release. No peer source outputs, other debates, prior assessments, calculated totals, winners, publication, biographies, network, paid calls, credentials, environment or session inspection, or subagents. Anonymous option labels must not be treated as pass identities. This is procedural isolation, not a filesystem sandbox.

Resolve only the packet’s disputes, exactly once in packet order. Choose option-a or option-b from each supplied pair. Do not average, invent a third option, change an undisputed field, change inventory structure, calculate totals or infer a winner. Select on source evidence and the frozen rubric; do not prefer compromise or one label in general. Required audio evidence must already be resolved before this context runs.

Motion: Did Jesus rise from the dead? Pro: David Wood. Con: Shabir Ally.

Assess only the formal opening speeches, both rebuttal rounds, crossfire and both five-minute closings, approximately 21:14–2:15:57. Exclude introduction, moderator logistics, applause, all subsequent audience Q&A, final replies to an audience question and outro. Neither speaker receives credit, penalty, inferred concession or failure-to-reply inference from excluded material.

The motion is Did Jesus rise from the dead? David Wood affirms death followed by resurrection. Shabir Ally challenges death and the historical reliability of resurrection narratives, and proposes survival followed by divine ascension as a compatible alternative. Ally does not defend substitution theory; he explicitly sees no historical reason for another person replacing Jesus. Do not score him as defending that rejected view or as rejecting all miracles.

Ally distinguishes historical reconstruction, Quranic interpretation and personal faith. He allows diverse Muslim interpretations of being raised, acknowledges that critical scholars reject Islam too, and explicitly concedes their widespread acceptance of Jesus’s death in his closing. His own disagreement concerns its warrant, including the conditional challenge if later physical appearances are accepted. Do not erase that concession, turn possibilities into demonstrated history, or impose a naturalistic burden he never adopts.

Wood uses the early 1 Corinthians creed, the claimed death consensus, reported appearances to individuals and groups, and sincerity under persecution. Preserve the difference between evidence of sincere belief and evidence that resurrection occurred. His opening attack on substitution precedes Ally’s explicit rejection; Wood subsequently develops different Quranic consistency challenges. Evaluate those actual stages rather than making his entire case depend on a view Ally disavows.

Ally’s development claim is expressly a trend, not a strictly linear increase in miracle counts. He acknowledges that Mark’s young man may be an angel and answers Wood’s numerical-miracle comparison by distinguishing quality and type. Preserve the geographic and narrative examples and their immediate qualifications. Book quotations, scholarly agreements, dating claims, medical claims and scriptural translations are the speakers’ evidence, not independently verified facts.

Distinguish postmortem vindication or assumption from rescue before death. Wood explicitly challenges the title of Daniel Smith’s work and quotes Acts 2 affirming death; Ally invokes older Psalm contexts, Q reconstruction, the sign of Jonah and later physical appearances. Do not assume one reported scholar’s view automatically endorses every step of either speaker’s inference.

Wood challenges consistent application of critical methods to both traditions and appeals to Quranic instructions about judging by the Gospel and true followers becoming uppermost. Ally responds with legal context, what God revealed therein, moral or spiritual victory, and the Quran’s instructional use of existing stories. His Hulk analogy illustrates literary reference, not a claim that Jesus is fictional. Wood’s comparison involving neo-Nazis is a claimed spectrum analogy, explicitly not literal affiliation; no extra penalty for tone or an invented literal allegation.

Automatic captions contain recognizer errors in names and technical terms, omitted Arabic words, rolling timestamp overlap and brief mixed interjections. Do not quote corrupted proper names or Arabic, infer missing arguments from recognition failures, or treat them as clarity defects. Use the clear surrounding source. Event2669 (zero-based) contains the end of Wood’s turn and start of Ally’s turn; never select that mixed event as a single-speaker move or quotation. Other uncertain selected passages must be marked below high confidence and audio-verified under the frozen protocol.

Five targeted automated audio checks supplement the captions: Wood’s opening core, Ally’s survival/ascension distinction, the first long handoff gap, the mixed crossfire handoff, and the closing-to-Q&A boundary. These are automated recognition cross-checks, not direct human listening. They do not replace canonical captions or excuse any new below-high-confidence inventory trigger.

Featured quotations must be exact complete 3–18 word thoughts with clear referents and any material qualification. Do not quote an opponent’s reported position as the speaker’s own conclusion. A source fragment must be replaced by another complete excerpt, never completed with invented words.

Completed audio-verification findings govern unresolved wording and attribution; they do not rewrite canonical exact captions or add substantive evidence.

Report any breach. resolutions exactly matches dispute order and count; each entry {disputeId,selectedOption,rationale}. selectedOption is option-a or option-b. Rationale is at least 40 characters and explains the specific source evidence and criterion supporting the choice.

        "startMs": 1557120,
        "endMs": 1682080,
        "excerpt": "The scholarly consensus on Jesus' death arises from one having a variety of ancient sources reporting that Jesus was crucified and two knowing how crucifixion works. We have Christian, Jewish and Roman sources reporting Jesus execution. Four gospels, letters of Paul, Clement of Rome, Polycarp, Josephus, Tacitus, Mara Sarapion, Lucian of Samasada and so on. And our understanding of Roman crucifixion rules out any speculations about Jesus surviving his execution. Today, we tend to think of crucifixion as just nailing someone to a cross. But Roman crucifixion was a three-step process. The first step was the scourging, which was sometimes called the half death because victims would be half dead by the time it was finished. The Romans used a flegrum made of leather thongs with chunks of bone or metal woven into the strands designed to remove human flesh. We have records of people being beaten until their veins and arteries were exposed, until their bones were showing, or until their intestines spilled out. And that was just the beginning. Step two was nailing the victim to a cross and letting him hang there while the blood drained out of him. Once he stopped gasping for breath, the Romans knew that their work was done almost. There was still the matter of what to do with the body. Generally, the Romans would leave a corpse on the cross and let it rot or be torn apart by wild jackals and birds. But if they needed to pry someone off a cross, they would stab him through the heart with a sword or a spear just to make sure he was dead. not the sort of process anyone's going to walk away from. So Jesus death isn't simply a point of Christian doctrine. It's a fact of history as even non-Christian scholars are happy to admit."

DOCUMENT docs/reassessment-rubric-v2.1.md
# Slugfester Reassessment Rubric v2.1

Score the argumentative performance present in the locked source—not whether a worldview is true, admirable, popular, or familiar. A score is earned move by move and has no automatic floor.

## Shared band anchors

| Band | Operational meaning |
| --- | --- |
| 95–100 | Exceptional and unusually complete: explicit inferential structure, strong public warrant, direct engagement with the strongest live reply, and little material repair needed. |
| 85–94 | Very strong: the move substantially advances its burden and survives the best live objection, with only bounded residual gaps. |
| 75–84 | Strong/competent: coherent and relevant, but compressed, incompletely warranted, or vulnerable at one important bridge. |
| 65–74 | Mixed: a useful core is present, yet a material premise, inference, or reply remains underdeveloped. |
| 50–64 | Weak: a major burden is not discharged, or the move largely misses the live issue despite some relevant content. |
| 25–49 | Very weak: the inference is seriously defective, substantially unsupported, or mostly unresponsive. |
| 0–24 | Non-performance: no usable argument on the assigned issue, a self-defeating contradiction, or content that is essentially irrelevant to the burden. |

Use 50, 70, 80, and 90 as decision boundaries, not default magnets. Before moving a dimension by five points, identify the particular transcript evidence or missing bridge that warrants the change.

## Dimensions

### Logical coherence — 25%

- **90+**: premises, intermediate steps, scope, and conclusion are explicit and mutually consistent; the strongest live validity challenge is answered.
- **75–89**: the inference is intelligible and mostly valid but leaves one bridge implicit or one ambiguity unresolved.
- **50–74**: the conclusion depends on a material equivocation, unsupported conditional, false dilemma, internal tension, or non sequitur.
- **Below 50**: the move is self-undermining, plainly invalid at its core, or lacks an inferential route to its conclusion.

Do not lower this dimension merely because a premise lacks evidence; that belongs primarily under evidence/warrant.

### Evidence and warrant — 20%

- **90+**: relevant evidence is specific, sourceable, representative, and connected to the conclusion through defended warrants and serious alternative comparison.
- **75–89**: appropriate support is offered, but documentation, independence, base rates, rival hypotheses, or a key warrant remains compressed.
- **50–74**: authority, anecdote, assertion, selective examples, or speculative mechanisms carry substantial weight without adequate checking.
- **Below 50**: a fact-dependent conclusion is effectively unsupported or relies on evidence contradicted within the locked record.

Lack of an inline citation is not itself disproof. Score the degree to which verification matters to the inference.

### Responsiveness — 20%

- **90+**: accurately identifies and answers the opponent's strongest relevant claim at its decisive premise or inference.
- **75–89**: directly engages the live issue but leaves a major component unanswered or answers it only conditionally.
- **50–74**: responds to a secondary point, a weaker reconstruction, or only one part of a compound objection.
- **Below 50**: evades, changes the subject, or attacks a position the opponent did not advance.

Constructive openings are scored for responsiveness to the motion and known burden, not penalized for lacking an earlier opponent move.

### Relevance and burden — 15%

- **90+**: directly discharges a locked burden or decisively defeats an opponent burden, with a conclusion proportionate to what that success establishes.
- **75–89**: materially advances the burden but does not complete it or leaves one required link unstated.
- **50–74**: related to the topic but peripheral, burden-shifting, or stronger in rhetoric than in motion-level consequence.
- **Below 50**: irrelevant to the locked motion or depends on assigning the opponent a burden they did not adopt.

Judge opportunity fairly. Do not invent a positive contrary burden for a side whose declared role is critical.

### Precision and clarity — 10%

- **90+**: key terms, modality, comparison class, scope, and confidence are stable and easy to audit.
- **75–89**: the move is understandable with only minor ambiguity or compression.
- **50–74**: unstable terminology, vague quantifiers, unclear referents, or missing distinctions materially obscure evaluation.
- **Below 50**: the claim cannot be reconstructed reliably enough to assess.

Fluency, wit, and polish are not clarity unless they improve the recoverable argument.

### Calibration and charity — 10%

- **90+**: confidence matches the evidence, concessions are accurate, alternatives are represented in their strongest live form, and uncertainty is explicit.
- **75–89**: generally proportionate and fair, with limited overstatement or incomplete treatment of an alternative.
- **50–74**: significant overclaiming, selective skepticism, motive attribution, or an avoidably weak representation of the alternative.
- **Below 50**: certainty is radically disproportionate or the move depends on a gross caricature.

Do not award charity merely for politeness or deduct it for an abrasive tone unless the tone changes the argumentative representation.

## Penalty controls

- Identify one primary home for each defect. Apply effects in additional dimensions only when the transcript shows genuinely distinct consequences.
- Compatibility is not explanation; possibility can answer contradiction without establishing probability.
- Familiar named arguments receive no pedigree bonus. Score only the version actually presented.
- A fallacy or bias tag never creates an extra deduction.
- Audience response, status, accent, speed, and confidence are excluded.
- Missing replies are represented by response links and burden completion; do not manufacture paired moves.
- Score diagnostic questions as questions or challenges, not as unargued contrary theories.

## Aggregation

Move scores use fixed dimension weights. Section scores are rounded importance-weighted means of selected moves. Overall scores are prelocked section-weighted means plus a −5 to +5 burden-completion adjustment. No additional coverage, coherence, resilience, or global-calibration meta-score is added; those qualities are already visible in the moves and burden record.

See [`assessment-workflow-v2.1.md`](assessment-workflow-v2.1.md) for formulas, disagreement thresholds, provenance, and promotion gates.

END DOCUMENT

DOCUMENT docs/assessment-production/standalone-debates-v1/debate-301/source/assessment-source-notes.md
# Assessment source notes

Assess only the formal opening speeches, both rebuttal rounds, crossfire and both five-minute closings, approximately 21:14–2:15:57. Exclude introduction, moderator logistics, applause, all subsequent audience Q&A, final replies to an audience question and outro. Neither speaker receives credit, penalty, inferred concession or failure-to-reply inference from excluded material.

The motion is Did Jesus rise from the dead? David Wood affirms death followed by resurrection. Shabir Ally challenges death and the historical reliability of resurrection narratives, and proposes survival followed by divine ascension as a compatible alternative. Ally does not defend substitution theory; he explicitly sees no historical reason for another person replacing Jesus. Do not score him as defending that rejected view or as rejecting all miracles.

Ally distinguishes historical reconstruction, Quranic interpretation and personal faith. He allows diverse Muslim interpretations of being raised, acknowledges that critical scholars reject Islam too, and explicitly concedes their widespread acceptance of Jesus’s death in his closing. His own disagreement concerns its warrant, including the conditional challenge if later physical appearances are accepted. Do not erase that concession, turn possibilities into demonstrated history, or impose a naturalistic burden he never adopts.

Wood uses the early 1 Corinthians creed, the claimed death consensus, reported appearances to individuals and groups, and sincerity under persecution. Preserve the difference between evidence of sincere belief and evidence that resurrection occurred. His opening attack on substitution precedes Ally’s explicit rejection; Wood subsequently develops different Quranic consistency challenges. Evaluate those actual stages rather than making his entire case depend on a view Ally disavows.

Ally’s development claim is expressly a trend, not a strictly linear increase in miracle counts. He acknowledges that Mark’s young man may be an angel and answers Wood’s numerical-miracle comparison by distinguishing quality and type. Preserve the geographic and narrative examples and their immediate qualifications. Book quotations, scholarly agreements, dating claims, medical claims and scriptural translations are the speakers’ evidence, not independently verified facts.

Distinguish postmortem vindication or assumption from rescue before death. Wood explicitly challenges the title of Daniel Smith’s work and quotes Acts 2 affirming death; Ally invokes older Psalm contexts, Q reconstruction, the sign of Jonah and later physical appearances. Do not assume one reported scholar’s view automatically endorses every step of either speaker’s inference.

Wood challenges consistent application of critical methods to both traditions and appeals to Quranic instructions about judging by the Gospel and true followers becoming uppermost. Ally responds with legal context, what God revealed therein, moral or spiritual victory, and the Quran’s instructional use of existing stories. His Hulk analogy illustrates literary reference, not a claim that Jesus is fictional. Wood’s comparison involving neo-Nazis is a claimed spectrum analogy, explicitly not literal affiliation; no extra penalty for tone or an invented literal allegation.

Automatic captions contain recognizer errors in names and technical terms, omitted Arabic words, rolling timestamp overlap and brief mixed interjections. Do not quote corrupted proper names or Arabic, infer missing arguments from recognition failures, or treat them as clarity defects. Use the clear surrounding source. Event2669 (zero-based) contains the end of Wood’s turn and start of Ally’s turn; never select that mixed event as a single-speaker move or quotation. Other uncertain selected passages must be marked below high confidence and audio-verified under the frozen protocol.

Five targeted automated audio checks supplement the captions: Wood’s opening core, Ally’s survival/ascension distinction, the first long handoff gap, the mixed crossfire handoff, and the closing-to-Q&A boundary. These are automated recognition cross-checks, not direct human listening. They do not replace canonical captions or excuse any new below-high-confidence inventory trigger.

Featured quotations must be exact complete 3–18 word thoughts with clear referents and any material qualification. Do not quote an opponent’s reported position as the speaker’s own conclusion. A source fragment must be replaced by another complete excerpt, never completed with invented words.

SHARED b29b83f02fcf678be999e0eb24992e7f77590dde9de96ae9f3c2cd2234ec8c05
This passage eliminates two skeptical responses to Jesus resur res resurrection. The legend hypothesis and the hallucination hypothesis. At the internet level, there are still people who claim that belief in Jesus resurrection came about over a period of legendary development over several decades. But this is factually false. We know what the earliest Christian preaching was. And this goes back to the very beginning. You also may have heard people argue that Jesus disciples simply hallucinated the resurrection appearances. But a hallucination by definition is something that occurs in the mind of the person who's seeing it. Since Jesus appeared to entire groups on multiple occasions, hallucinations just can't explain the appearances. So we can't attribute the resurrection appearances to legend or to hallucinations.
END SHARED
